### 🤖 Assistant
*2026-08-18 12:50:46*

You are helping develop a Java Swing application that follows the Clean Architecture style used in the CACoding repository.

Architecture Rules

The package structure is:

app
data_access
entity
interface_adapter
use_case
view

Use package-by-layer, not package-by-feature.

The data_access, entity, view, and app packages do not have use-case-specific subpackages.

The use_case and interface_adapter packages contain subpackages for individual use cases such as:

login
signup
logged_in

Follow existing repository conventions before introducing new abstractions.

Layer Responsibilities

entity
- Contains enterprise business entities.
- No Swing dependencies.
- No UI logic.
- No data_access dependencies.

use_case
- Contains InputBoundary interfaces.
- Contains OutputBoundary interfaces.
- Contains Interactors.
- Contains RequestModel classes.
- Contains ResponseModel classes.
- Contains use-case-specific business rules.
- Depends only on entities and boundary interfaces.

interface_adapter
- Contains Controllers.
- Contains Presenters.
- Contains ViewModels.
- Converts between UI data and use-case data.
- Never contains business rules.

view
- Contains Swing views and panels.
- Calls Controllers.
- Reads ViewModels.
- Never directly calls Interactors.

data_access
- Implements gateway or repository interfaces.
- Performs persistence work.
- Knows about storage details.

app
- Wires the application together.
- Creates views, controllers, presenters, interactors, and repositories.
- Performs dependency injection.

Naming Conventions

For a use case named "foo":

use_case.foo
    FooInputBoundary
    FooOutputBoundary
    FooInteractor
    FooRequestModel
    FooResponseModel

interface_adapter.foo
    FooController
    FooPresenter
    FooViewModel
    FooState

view
    FooView

Use the exact naming style already present in the project.

Coding Conventions

- Use Java 21 features when they improve clarity.
- Prefer constructor injection.
- Avoid static mutable state.
- Keep classes small and focused.
- Preserve existing package names.
- Preserve existing naming conventions.
- Generate code that resembles the surrounding codebase.
- Do not introduce frameworks.
- Do not introduce Spring, Guice, Lombok, JavaFX, or other external architectural frameworks unless explicitly requested.

When Adding a New Use Case

Always generate:

1. RequestModel
2. ResponseModel
3. InputBoundary
4. OutputBoundary
5. Interactor
6. Controller
7. Presenter
8. ViewModel updates
9. App wiring changes
10. Swing view integration

Before generating code:
- Identify which layer each class belongs to.
- Explain dependencies between classes.
- Verify that dependencies point inward according to Clean Architecture.

When reviewing code:
- Flag any dependency from entity -> use_case.
- Flag any dependency from use_case -> Swing.
- Flag any dependency from view -> interactor.
- Flag any dependency from presenter -> view.
- Flag any dependency that violates Clean Architecture.

Default Goal

Produce maintainable code that would fit naturally into the CACoding repository and be acceptable in a second-year software design course emphasizing Clean Architecture. When making changes, first describe the files that need modification and why. Then generate only the minimal set of changes required.

2. passwords don't match → prepareFailView("Passwords don't match.")
3. success → create user, save, prepareSuccessView with outputData

Also switchToLoginView() delegates to presenter.

1. userAlreadyExists_callsPrepareFailView - when existsByName returns true, verify prepareFailView with "User already exists.", verify save never called
2. passwordsDoNotMatch_callsPrepareFailView - existsByName false, passwords differ → prepareFailView "Passwords don't match."
3. success_callsPrepareSuccessViewAndSaves - existsByName false, passwords match → userFactory.create returns user, save called, prepareSuccessView called with username and useCaseFailed false
4. switchToLoginView_delegatesToPresenter

I'll use @ExtendWith(MockitoExtension.class), @Mock for the DAO, presenter, factory. Use @Captor or ArgumentCaptor to check the SignupOutputData fields.

Let me analyze the tests for possible improvements. Key observations:

**Issues across all tests:**

1. **Mocking the `User` entity / using mocks for values** — In `signupSuccess`, `user.getName()` is stubbed to return "Paul", then `assertEquals("Paul", outputData.getUsername())` verifies the *mock's* stub, not real behavior. If someone changed the interactor to pass a hardcoded username, the test wouldn't catch it. Better: use a real `User` (e.g., `CommonUser`) instead of mocking it. `CommonUser` is a concrete entity. Using a real entity avoids "mock the thing you're testing a behavior of" and makes the assertion meaningful. This is a classic improvement: prefer real objects for entities, mocks only for boundaries.

2. **Redundant stubbing / verify duplication** — In `signupSuccess`, `when(userFactory.create(...))` then `verify(userFactory).create(...)` duplicates the interaction check but with mocks it's testing the mock. With a real UserFactory (CommonUserFactory), the create call would actually create a real user and `user.getName()` would return real value.

3. **Login uses `user.getPassword()` mock** — the interactor calls `userDataAccessObject.get(username).getPassword()`. Using a real User entity would make password matching a genuine behavior check.

4. **Strict stubbing** — MockitoExtension defaults to strict stubs, so unused stubs fail. That's fine. But `@Mock User` requires stubs for getName etc.

5. **Test for `switchToLoginView`** — fine, it's a delegation test.

6. **Missing test coverage:**
   - Signup: no test that `prepareSuccessView` is NOT called on failure paths (could add `verify(userPresenter, never()).prepareSuccessView(any())`). Also the success test doesn't verify the presenter's fail view is never called.
   - Login: could verify `prepareSuccessView` never called on failures.
   - Login success: the interactor calls `userDataAccessObject.get()` twice; not a problem.
   - ChangePassword: `prepareFailView` is on the boundary but the interactor never calls it — the test only covers the happy path. There's no failure path in the interactor, so it's fine.

7. **`any()` matcher usage** — In signup failure tests, `verify(userDataAccessObject, never()).save(any())`. Could use `never()` without args. Minor.

8. **Naming**: method names follow convention fine.

9. **`@Mock User` and stub of `getName`/`getPassword`** — the biggest improvement is replacing `@Mock User` with real `CommonUser` and `@Mock UserFactory` with real `CommonUserFactory`. That makes assertions meaningful.

Key changes from the current package-by-layer layout:

- **Components at top**: `login`, `signup`, `change_password` each contain their full vertical slice (controller → interactor → view), so the architecture "screams" the domain.
- **Splits to consolidate**: `ChangePasswordController`, `ChangePasswordPresenter`, `ChangePasswordViewModel` move from `interface_adapter.logged_in` into `change_password`; the change-password panel moves out of `LoggedInView`.
- **Only genuinely shared, non-feature code stays separate**: the `User` entity, the three DAOs (shared across features via interfaces), reusable view plumbing (`ViewModel`, `ViewManager`, `LabelTextPanel`), and the `app` composition root.
- `logged_in` disappears as a layer package — its remaining glue either folds into `change_password` or the `LoggedInView` home panel.

Dependency direction stays the same (feature code depends inward on `user`/`data_access`, `app` wires everything); only the grouping changes.

### 🤖 Assistant
*2026-08-18 12:50:46*

💭 **Reasoning:**

<details>
<summary>Click to expand reasoning</summary>

The user is asking a genuine question about industry practice for screaming architecture vs package-by-component. This is a knowledge/opinion question. I should answer concisely but this warrants a bit of detail. Not a coding task.

Let me answer honestly and concisely.

</details>

It's an honest mix — both exist in the wild. A few points:

**Screaming architecture, as Simon Brown describes it, is actually a *simplified* teaching device.** His point is precisely that you drop the layer subpackages and let the capability be the unit. So the flat tree I gave you is a faithful realization of the "missing chapter."

**In real production code, capabilities usually aren't that flat.** You'd typically see some internal structure. Common refinements:

- **A root package prefix** — industry code almost never sits at the top-level Java namespace. It'd be `com.company.product.login.*`, etc.
- **Layer subpackages *inside* each capability** when a capability grows — e.g. `login/controller`, `login/usecase`, `login/presenter`, `login/view`. This is a pragmatic hybrid: feature-first at the top, layer within.
- **`internal` / `api` split** in each component — public interface surface vs implementation details, often enforced via Java module `exports` (this is more of a module-system thing than package layout).
- **Aggregate-root or bounded-context grouping** — capabilities cluster around domain aggregates, not one-off use cases, so a "capability" maps to a cluster of use cases rather than a single one.

**In practice**: most mature codebases I've seen lean feature/domain-first at the top with some internal layering, *because* that's what screams the domain and keeps teams working in isolated areas. The pure layer-based (your current layout) is very common in teaching and in older/enterprise-constrained code.

**For a course project**, the flat screaming-architecture version is the better answer — it directly demonstrates the idea and the missing-chapter argument. If you wanted to hedge for realism, adding a root package prefix (e.g. `edu.csc207.ca.<capability>`) and optional per-capability layer subpackages would be the "industry" flavor of it.

So: not flat in production, but flat is the intended essence of the pattern.

You're right, and that's a better take on it. If the *learning objective* is the layered architecture — where each layer belongs, how dependencies point inward, what a controller vs. presenter vs. interactor does — then a flat layout actively obscures the very thing students are supposed to see and be graded on.

The two layouts teach different things:

- **Package-by-layer** (your current one): the layers are visible at a glance, and the directory tree *is* the dependency diagram. It makes the architecture the subject of study.
- **Screaming architecture**: the domain is the subject; layers are deliberately hidden. That's a design-shaping concern, not a learning concern.

My "for a course project" framing was off — it conflated "simpler" with "better," and it ignored who the audience is. The right question isn't "which is more realistic" but "what is the exercise meant to demonstrate." If the course is about layered/clean architecture, package-by-layer is the correct choice, and flat screaming architecture would be a regression for the students' understanding.

So I'd revise my recommendation: keep your current package-by-layer layout for the course. Screaming architecture is worth *discussing* as an alternative (and it's a genuinely useful pattern), but as the deliverable it would work against the curriculum's goal.

Let me think about this honestly. This is an architectural discussion question, not a coding task. I should give my genuine opinion.

Arguments for a "shared" folder:
- Groups non-feature code together
- Signals these are cross-cutting

Arguments against:
- "shared" is a dumping ground — it doesn't communicate *what* the shared things are or *why* they're shared
- It conflates very different concerns: entities (domain), persistence (infrastructure), UI plumbing (framework)
- The name "shared" says nothing about dependency rules — the whole point of the layered structure is that dependencies point inward. A flat "shared" obscures the layer structure, which the user themselves said they care about (they want layers visible for teaching)
- The dependency direction matters: entity is depended on by everything; data_access implements interfaces defined by use cases; framework is used by views. These have different roles.

My recommendation: instead of "shared", keep the meaningful names: `user` (or `domain`/`entity`), `data_access`, `framework`/`infrastructure`. These names communicate roles, not just "sharedness". Alternatively, one could argue "shared" is fine for a toy. But better: keep semantically meaningful top-level packages.

Actually there's a middle ground: a "shared" grouping is fine if you think the user wants fewer top-level folders. But I'd push back gently: the concern with "shared" is it's a catch-all without semantic content; the dependency rules (entity → no deps; data_access → implements use case interfaces) are the teaching point, and those are clearer when the layers keep their distinct names.

But also — in this specific architecture, do data_access classes actually need to be "shared"? They implement per-use-case interfaces (SignupUserDataAccessInterface etc.), so they're shared across features by virtue of implementing multiple interfaces. The entity is genuinely shared. Framework/UI plumbing is shared.

I should give a concise, honest opinion with a recommendation. Keep it short. Maybe 2-3 paragraphs. Let me not over-elaborate.

I'll say: "shared" works but is a weak name; prefer distinct top-level names that communicate role and dependency position. Then show the option. Keep it brief.

</details>

I'd push back on "shared" as a top-level name. It's accurate but it communicates almost nothing — it's a catch-all, and it groups things that have *very different roles* and *different dependency positions*:

- `user` is the domain core — everything depends on it, it depends on nothing.
- `data_access` is infrastructure that *implements* interfaces the use cases define; the dependency arrow points the other way (use_case → data_access is wrong, data_access → use_case interface is right).
- `framework` is UI plumbing used by views.

That's precisely the layer structure you want students to see. Folding all three under one `shared/` hides it, same problem as flat screaming architecture, just one level down.

Two alternatives I'd prefer:

1. **Keep the distinct names at the top level** (my earlier tree): `user/`, `data_access/`, `framework/`. Each name states its role; the layering stays readable.
2. If you want fewer top-level folders and a "common/kitchen-sink" feel is acceptable for a toy: `shared/user`, `shared/data_access`, `shared/framework` — *subfolders* that keep semantic names inside. You get the grouping signal without losing the role names.

The version to avoid is `shared/` with everything dumped flat inside it. The name does the work of saying "these are shared"; it doesn't say *why* or *how they relate*.

My vote: option 1 for this project — three small top-level folders is hardly clutter, and the layer names do real teaching work. What do you think?

Standard practices:

2. 50/72 rule from git book / Tim Pope's "A Note About Git Commit Messages": imperative mood, capitalize, short summary < 50 chars, body wrapped at 72, explain what and why, not how.
3. GitHub style — less strict.

Standard practice is mostly Converging on two conventions:

1. **Conventional Commits** (`type(scope): summary`) — machine-readable, enables automatic changelogs and versioning (semantic-release). Types: `feat`, `fix`, `refactor`, `test`, `build`, `docs`, `chore`, etc.
2. **Tim Pope's / git-book rules** — imperative mood, capitalized, summary ≤50 chars, body wrapped at 72, body explains *why* not *what*.

### 🤖 Assistant
*2026-08-18 12:50:46*

*[step-start part]*

💭 **Reasoning:**

<details>
<summary>Click to expand reasoning</summary>

The user is asking a design question: is signup an account capability or an authentication capability? In our current structure, signup is under authentication (with login), and change_password is under account.

This is a discussion question — answer concisely with reasoning, no code changes. Give a genuine opinion.

Common industry view: signup is account lifecycle (provisioning), login is authentication (verifying identity). Change password is account management. So arguably signup belongs in account, not authentication. But signup also has the authentication component (creating credentials). There's no single right answer; it's about cohesion and how the domain thinks of it.

Some views:
- Authentication = verifying credentials (login, logout, refresh tokens). Signup/registration is identity provisioning — often owned by an "accounts" or "users" service, not the auth service.
- In many systems, signup is part of account management (creating an account) while login is authentication.
- Tight coupling: signup in this codebase switches to the login view (SignupPresenter → LoginViewModel), so they're adjacent in flow. That's UI flow cohesion, not domain cohesion.

My honest opinion: signup leans account (it's about creating/managing an account), login is authentication. The flow adjacency (signup → login) is a UI flow thing, not capability cohesion. So I'd say account. But it's defensible either way; consistency matters more than the specific answer.

Keep it short.

</details>

Honest opinion: signup is more of an **account** thing, not authentication.

- **Authentication** is verifying identity: login, logout, token refresh, sessions.
- **Signup** is *provisioning* identity — creating the account and its credentials. That's account lifecycle work, closer to change_password (account management) than to login.

In industry, registration/identity-provisioning usually lives in the accounts/user service, while authentication is its own concern (often a separate auth service).

The only reason signup sits next to login here is *flow*: signing up leads to the login screen (your `SignupPresenter` switches to `LoginViewModel`). That's UI-flow adjacency, not domain cohesion — a weak reason to group them.

So I'd argue `account/{signup, change_password, logged_in}` and `authentication/login` alone. But both groupings are defensible; what matters more is that the grouping reflects a real domain boundary you can articulate — and either way, "because they're on the same screen" is the one answer to avoid.

Simple version — rebase replays your commits *on top of* main's latest, producing a straight line instead of a merge knot. The standard flow:

**1. Push the branch and open the PR**
```
git push -u origin capability-packages
```
On GitHub: PR from `capability-packages` → `main`.

**2. While the PR is open, keep it current with main** (industry norm — before merging, rebase onto latest main):
```
git fetch origin
git rebase origin/main
git push --force-with-lease
```
This un-applies your 4 commits, moves the branch tip to main's newest commit, and re-applies your commits on top. History stays linear. (`--force-with-lease` is the safe force-push — it refuses if someone else touched the branch.)

**3. Merge the PR on GitHub** — click the dropdown next to the merge button and pick **"Rebase and merge"** instead of "Create a merge commit". GitHub replays your commits onto main and fast-forwards main to them. Result on GitHub:

```
main:        ... A -- B -- C -- D      (your 4 commits, straight line)
```

**4. Clean up locally**
```
git checkout main
git pull --ff-only        # fast-forwards main to the merged commits
git branch -d capability-packages
```

The mental model: *merge* = "bring the two histories together" (a knot); *rebase* = "pretend I always started from the latest main" (a straight line). One caution: rebasing rewrites commit hashes, so it's only for commits you haven't shared with others — which is exactly your situation here.

### 🤖 Assistant
*2026-08-18 12:50:46*

*[step-start part]*

💭 **Reasoning:**

<details>
<summary>Click to expand reasoning</summary>

A genuine question about industry practice. Give a concise, honest answer. No tools needed.

</details>

For GitHub-based teams, the most common setup is "merge commits on main" with the PRs themselves being **squash-merged** (GitHub's default), not rebase-merged. So: rebase-merge is common but not the most common.

Rough picture:

- **Squash and merge** — most popular on GitHub. The whole PR becomes one commit on main ("Add feature X"). Keeps main history a clean linear series of feature-sized commits. GitHub's default for a reason.
- **Rebase and merge** — common in projects that want each commit in the PR preserved individually with linear history (e.g., reviewers want to see the commit-by-commit development). Your 4-commit PR is a natural fit.
- **Merge commit** — still dominant in larger/Gerrit-style or enterprise workflows where the merge record matters (audit trail of what landed when), and effectively required on very busy main branches.

The broader trend (trunk-based development, "always rebase before merging") makes *rebase-as-a-workflow* — pulling latest main and replaying your work before merging — nearly universal regardless of which merge button you click. So rebasing before merge: industry standard. Rebasing *as the merge mechanism*: common but second to squash.

A very defensible modern default: squash on GitHub, but you rebase locally to stay current before opening/merging the PR.

For your current situation, `main` hasn't advanced since you branched, so it would be a no-op — but here's the flow for when it has:

```bash
git checkout capability-packages        # get on your feature branch
git fetch origin                        # pick up the latest state of the remote
git rebase origin/main                  # replay your commits on top of latest main
```

What happens: git takes your 4 commits off the branch tip, moves the branch pointer to `origin/main`'s newest commit, then re-applies your commits one at a time. If a commit conflicts with main's changes, git stops and tells you — you resolve the files, `git add` them, and continue with `git rebase --continue`. If things go sideways, `git rebase --abort` returns you to where you were.

After a rebase, your local commits have new hashes, so any push to the remote branch needs:

```bash
git push --force-with-lease
```

In your case today: `git checkout capability-packages && git fetch origin && git rebase origin/main` would just say "Current branch is up to date" — main hasn't moved since `1c406c8`.

```
refactor: organize packages by business capability
```

Body:

```
## What

Rework the package layout from package-by-layer to package-by-capability
(Screaming Architecture). Each business capability is a top-level package
with its use cases and layers inside:

- `account/signup`, `account/change_password`, `authentication/login`
- `user/`, `data_access/`, `framework/`, `app/` for shared concerns

## Why

Layers were the only visible structure (entity, use_case,
interface_adapter, view), which hides what the program does. Grouping by
capability makes the domain legible while preserving the layered
dependency rules inside each use case.

## Changes

- Merge `logged_in` into `change_password` (LoggedIn* -> ChangePassword*)
- Move `signup` under `account` (account provisioning, not authentication)
- Rename `entity` -> `user`; shared UI plumbing into `framework/`
- Make `LabelTextPanel` public (was package-private; latent bug surfaced
  by the package moves)

## Verification

- `mvn clean test`: 8 tests, 0 failures
- Added JUnit 5 + Mockito and unit tests for all three interactors

## Note

Choose **Rebase and merge** to keep the 4 commits linear on main.
```

The commits themselves are already conventional and will show up in the PR — since you're switching to rebase-and-merge, they'll land on `main` as-is rather than being squashed.

Body:

```
## What

Rework the package layout from package-by-layer to package-by-capability
(Screaming Architecture). Each business capability is a top-level package
with its use cases and layers inside:

- `account/signup`, `account/change_password`, `authentication/login`
- `user/`, `data_access/`, `framework/`, `app/` for shared concerns

## Why

Layers were the only visible structure (entity, use_case,
interface_adapter, view), which hides what the program does. Grouping by
capability makes the domain legible while preserving the layered
dependency rules inside each use case.

## Changes

- Merge `logged_in` into `change_password` (LoggedIn* -> ChangePassword*)
- Move `signup` under `account` (account provisioning, not authentication)
- Rename `entity` -> `user`; shared UI plumbing into `framework/`
- Make `LabelTextPanel` public (was package-private; latent bug surfaced
  by the package moves)

## New tests

This PR also introduces the project's first automated test suite:

- Add JUnit 5 and Mockito dependencies (replacing JUnit 4)
- New unit tests covering success and failure paths of the
  `SignupInteractor`, `LoginInteractor`, and `ChangePasswordInteractor`

## Verification

- `mvn clean test`: 8 tests, 0 failures (newly added)
```

```
# Intro

Get started with OpenCode.

[**OpenCode**](/) is an open source AI coding agent. It’s available as a terminal-based interface, desktop app, or IDE extension.

![OpenCode TUI with the opencode theme](/docs/_astro/screenshot.CQjBbRyJ_1dLadc.webp)

Let’s get started.

---

#### [Prerequisites](#prerequisites)

To use OpenCode in your terminal, you’ll need:

1.  A modern terminal emulator like:
    
    -   [WezTerm](https://wezterm.org), cross-platform
    -   [Alacritty](https://alacritty.org), cross-platform
    -   [Ghostty](https://ghostty.org), Linux and macOS
    -   [Kitty](https://sw.kovidgoyal.net/kitty/), Linux and macOS
2.  API keys for the LLM providers you want to use.
    

---

## [Install](#install)

The easiest way to install OpenCode is through the install script.

Terminal window

```
curl -fsSL https://opencode.ai/install | bash
```

You can also install it with the following commands:

-   **Using Node.js**
    
    -   [npm](#tab-panel-0)
    -   [Bun](#tab-panel-1)
    -   [pnpm](#tab-panel-2)
    -   [Yarn](#tab-panel-3)
    
    Terminal window
    
    ```
    npm install -g opencode-ai
    ```
    
    Terminal window
    
    ```
    bun install -g opencode-ai
    ```
    
    Terminal window
    
    ```
    pnpm install -g opencode-ai
    ```
    
    Terminal window
    
    ```
    yarn global add opencode-ai
    ```
    
-   **Using Homebrew on macOS and Linux**
    
    Terminal window
    
    ```
    brew install anomalyco/tap/opencode
    ```
    
    > We recommend using the OpenCode tap for the most up to date releases. The official `brew install opencode` formula is maintained by the Homebrew team and is updated less frequently.
    
-   **Installing on Arch Linux**
    
    Terminal window
    
    ```
    sudo pacman -S opencode           # Arch Linux (Stable)paru -S opencode-bin              # Arch Linux (Latest from AUR)
    ```
    

#### [Windows](#windows)

Recommended: Use WSL

For the best experience on Windows, we recommend using [Windows Subsystem for Linux (WSL)](/docs/windows-wsl). It provides better performance and full compatibility with OpenCode’s features.

-   **Using Chocolatey**
    
    Terminal window
    
    ```
    choco install opencode
    ```
    
-   **Using Scoop**
    
    Terminal window
    
    ```
    scoop install opencode
    ```
    
-   **Using NPM**
    
    Terminal window
    
    ```
    npm install -g opencode-ai
    ```
    
-   **Using Mise**
    
    Terminal window
    
    ```
    mise use -g github:anomalyco/opencode
    ```
    
-   **Using Docker**
    
    Terminal window
    
    ```
    docker run -it --rm ghcr.io/anomalyco/opencode
    ```
    

Support for installing OpenCode on Windows using Bun is currently in progress.

You can also grab the binary from the [Releases](https://github.com/anomalyco/opencode/releases).

---

## [Configure](#configure)

With OpenCode you can use any LLM provider by configuring their API keys.

If you are new to using LLM providers, we recommend using [OpenCode Zen](/docs/zen). It’s a curated list of models that have been tested and verified by the OpenCode team.

1.  Run the `/connect` command in the TUI, select opencode, and head to [opencode.ai/auth](https://opencode.ai/auth).
    
    ```
    /connect
    ```
    
2.  Sign in, add your billing details, and copy your API key.
    
3.  Paste your API key.
    
    ```
    ┌ API key││└ enter
    ```
    

Alternatively, you can select one of the other providers. [Learn more](/docs/providers#directory).

---

## [Initialize](#initialize)

Now that you’ve configured a provider, you can navigate to a project that you want to work on.

Terminal window

```
cd /path/to/project
```

And run OpenCode.

Terminal window

```
opencode
```

Next, initialize OpenCode for the project by running the following command.

```
/init
```

This will get OpenCode to analyze your project and create an `AGENTS.md` file in the project root.

Tip

You should commit your project’s `AGENTS.md` file to Git.

This helps OpenCode understand the project structure and the coding patterns used.

---

## [Usage](#usage)

You are now ready to use OpenCode to work on your project. Feel free to ask it anything!

If you are new to using an AI coding agent, here are some examples that might help.

---

### [Ask questions](#ask-questions)

You can ask OpenCode to explain the codebase to you.

Tip

Use the `@` key to fuzzy search for files in the project.

```
How is authentication handled in @packages/functions/src/api/index.ts
```

This is helpful if there’s a part of the codebase that you didn’t work on.

---

### [Add features](#add-features)

You can ask OpenCode to add new features to your project. Though we first recommend asking it to create a plan.

1.  **Create a plan**
    
    OpenCode has a *Plan mode* that disables its ability to make changes and instead suggest *how* it’ll implement the feature.
    
    Switch to it using the **Tab** key. You’ll see an indicator for this in the lower right corner.
    
    ```
    <TAB>
    ```
    
    Now let’s describe what we want it to do.
    
    ```
    When a user deletes a note, we'd like to flag it as deleted in the database.Then create a screen that shows all the recently deleted notes.From this screen, the user can undelete a note or permanently delete it.
    ```
    
    You want to give OpenCode enough details to understand what you want. It helps to talk to it like you are talking to a junior developer on your team.
    
    Tip
    
    Give OpenCode plenty of context and examples to help it understand what you want.
    
2.  **Iterate on the plan**
    
    Once it gives you a plan, you can give it feedback or add more details.
    
    ```
    We'd like to design this new screen using a design I've used before.[Image #1] Take a look at this image and use it as a reference.
    ```
    
    Tip
    
    Drag and drop images into the terminal to add them to the prompt.
    
    OpenCode can scan any images you give it and add them to the prompt. You can do this by dragging and dropping an image into the terminal.
    
3.  **Build the feature**
    
    Once you feel comfortable with the plan, switch back to *Build mode* by hitting the **Tab** key again.
    
    ```
    <TAB>
    ```
    
    And asking it to make the changes.
    
    ```
    Sounds good! Go ahead and make the changes.
    ```
    

---

### [Make changes](#make-changes)

For more straightforward changes, you can ask OpenCode to directly build it without having to review the plan first.

```
We need to add authentication to the /settings route. Take a look at how this ishandled in the /notes route in @packages/functions/src/notes.ts and implementthe same logic in @packages/functions/src/settings.ts
```

You want to make sure you provide a good amount of detail so OpenCode makes the right changes.

---

### [Undo changes](#undo-changes)

Let’s say you ask OpenCode to make some changes.

```
Can you refactor the function in @packages/functions/src/api/index.ts?
```

But you realize that it is not what you wanted. You **can undo** the changes using the `/undo` command.

```
/undo
```

OpenCode will now revert the changes you made and show your original message again.

```
Can you refactor the function in @packages/functions/src/api/index.ts?
```

From here you can tweak the prompt and ask OpenCode to try again.

Tip

You can run `/undo` multiple times to undo multiple changes.

Or you **can redo** the changes using the `/redo` command.

```
/redo
```

---

## [Share](#share)

The conversations that you have with OpenCode can be [shared with your team](/docs/share).

```
/share
```

This will create a link to the current conversation and copy it to your clipboard.

Note

Conversations are not shared by default.

Here’s an [example conversation](https://opencode.ai/s/4XP1fce5) with OpenCode.

---

## [Customize](#customize)

And that’s it! You are now a pro at using OpenCode.

To make it your own, we recommend [picking a theme](/docs/themes), [customizing the keybinds](/docs/keybinds), [configuring code formatters](/docs/formatters), [creating custom commands](/docs/commands), or playing around with the [OpenCode config](/docs/config).
```

```
# CLI

OpenCode CLI options and commands.

The OpenCode CLI by default starts the [TUI](/docs/tui) when run without any arguments.

Terminal window

```
opencode
```

But it also accepts commands as documented on this page. This allows you to interact with OpenCode programmatically.

Terminal window

```
opencode run "Explain how closures work in JavaScript"
```

---

### [tui](#tui)

Start the OpenCode terminal user interface.

Terminal window

```
opencode [project]
```

#### [Flags](#flags)

Flag

Short

Description

`--continue`

`-c`

Continue the last session

`--session`

`-s`

Session ID to continue

`--fork`

Fork the session when continuing (use with `--continue` or `--session`)

`--prompt`

Prompt to use

`--model`

`-m`

Model to use in the form of provider/model

`--agent`

Agent to use

`--auto`

Auto-approve permissions that are not explicitly denied

`--port`

Port to listen on

`--hostname`

Hostname to listen on

`--mdns`

Enable mDNS discovery

`--mdns-domain`

Custom mDNS domain name

`--cors`

Additional browser origin(s) to allow CORS

---

## [Commands](#commands)

The OpenCode CLI also has the following commands.

---

### [agent](#agent)

Manage agents for OpenCode.

Terminal window

```
opencode agent [command]
```

---

#### [create](#create)

Create a new agent with custom configuration.

Terminal window

```
opencode agent create
```

This command will guide you through creating a new agent with a custom system prompt and permission configuration. Anything you don’t allow is denied in the generated agent’s frontmatter.

#### [Flags](#flags-1)

Flag

Short

Description

`--path`

Directory to write the agent file to (defaults to global or `.opencode/agent` based on the prompt)

`--description`

What the agent should do

`--mode`

Agent mode: `all`, `primary`, or `subagent`

`--permissions`

Comma-separated list of permissions to allow (default: all). Available: `bash`, `read`, `edit`, `glob`, `grep`, `webfetch`, `task`, `todowrite`, `websearch`, `lsp`, `skill`. Anything omitted is denied. Alias: `--tools`

`--model`

`-m`

Model to use, in `provider/model` format

Passing all of `--path`, `--description`, `--mode`, and `--permissions` runs the command non-interactively.

---

#### [list](#list)

List all available agents.

Terminal window

```
opencode agent list
```

---

### [attach](#attach)

Attach a terminal to an already running OpenCode backend server started via `serve` or `web` commands.

Terminal window

```
opencode attach [url]
```

This allows using the TUI with a remote OpenCode backend. For example:

Terminal window

```
# Start the backend server for web/mobile accessopencode web --port 4096 --hostname 0.0.0.0
# In another terminal, attach the TUI to the running backendopencode attach http://10.20.30.40:4096
```

#### [Flags](#flags-2)

Flag

Short

Description

`--dir`

Working directory to start TUI in

`--continue`

`-c`

Continue the last session

`--session`

`-s`

Session ID to continue

`--fork`

Fork the session when continuing (use with `--continue` or `--session`)

`--password`

`-p`

Basic auth password (defaults to `OPENCODE_SERVER_PASSWORD`)

`--username`

`-u`

Basic auth username (defaults to `OPENCODE_SERVER_USERNAME` or `opencode`)

---

### [auth](#auth)

Command to manage credentials and login for providers.

Terminal window

```
opencode auth [command]
```

---

#### [login](#login)

OpenCode is powered by the provider list at [Models.dev](https://models.dev), so you can use `opencode auth login` to configure API keys for any provider you’d like to use. This is stored in `~/.local/share/opencode/auth.json`.

Terminal window

```
opencode auth login
```

When OpenCode starts up it loads the providers from the credentials file. And if there are any keys defined in your environments or a `.env` file in your project.

##### [Flags](#flags-3)

Flag

Short

Description

`--provider`

`-p`

Provider ID or name to log in to

`--method`

`-m`

Login method label to use, skipping method selection

---

#### [list](#list-1)

Lists all the authenticated providers as stored in the credentials file.

Terminal window

```
opencode auth list
```

Or the short version.

Terminal window

```
opencode auth ls
```

---

#### [logout](#logout)

Logs you out of a provider by clearing it from the credentials file.

Terminal window

```
opencode auth logout
```

---

### [github](#github)

Manage the GitHub agent for repository automation.

Terminal window

```
opencode github [command]
```

---

#### [install](#install)

Install the GitHub agent in your repository.

Terminal window

```
opencode github install
```

This sets up the necessary GitHub Actions workflow and guides you through the configuration process. [Learn more](/docs/github).

---

#### [run](#run)

Run the GitHub agent. This is typically used in GitHub Actions.

Terminal window

```
opencode github run
```

##### [Flags](#flags-4)

Flag

Description

`--event`

GitHub mock event to run the agent for

`--token`

GitHub personal access token

---

### [mcp](#mcp)

Manage Model Context Protocol servers.

Terminal window

```
opencode mcp [command]
```

---

#### [add](#add)

Add an MCP server to your configuration.

Terminal window

```
opencode mcp add
```

This command will guide you through adding either a local or remote MCP server.

---

#### [list](#list-2)

List all configured MCP servers and their connection status.

Terminal window

```
opencode mcp list
```

Or use the short version.

Terminal window

```
opencode mcp ls
```

---

#### [auth](#auth-1)

Authenticate with an OAuth-enabled MCP server.

Terminal window

```
opencode mcp auth [name]
```

If you don’t provide a server name, you’ll be prompted to select from available OAuth-capable servers.

You can also list OAuth-capable servers and their authentication status.

Terminal window

```
opencode mcp auth list
```

Or use the short version.

Terminal window

```
opencode mcp auth ls
```

---

#### [logout](#logout-1)

Remove OAuth credentials for an MCP server.

Terminal window

```
opencode mcp logout [name]
```

---

#### [debug](#debug)

Debug OAuth connection issues for an MCP server.

Terminal window

```
opencode mcp debug <name>
```

---

### [models](#models)

List all available models from configured providers.

Terminal window

```
opencode models [provider]
```

This command displays all models available across your configured providers in the format `provider/model`.

This is useful for figuring out the exact model name to use in [your config](/docs/config/).

You can optionally pass a provider ID to filter models by that provider.

Terminal window

```
opencode models anthropic
```

#### [Flags](#flags-5)

Flag

Description

`--refresh`

Refresh the models cache from models.dev

`--verbose`

Use more verbose model output (includes metadata like costs)

Use the `--refresh` flag to update the cached model list. This is useful when new models have been added to a provider and you want to see them in OpenCode.

Terminal window

```
opencode models --refresh
```

---

### [run](#run-1)

Run opencode in non-interactive mode by passing a prompt directly.

Terminal window

```
opencode run [message..]
```

This is useful for scripting, automation, or when you want a quick answer without launching the full TUI. For example.

Terminal window

```
opencode run Explain the use of context in Go
```

You can also attach to a running `opencode serve` instance to avoid MCP server cold boot times on every run:

Terminal window

```
# Start a headless server in one terminalopencode serve
# In another terminal, run commands that attach to itopencode run --attach http://localhost:4096 "Explain async/await in JavaScript"
```

#### [Flags](#flags-6)

Flag

Short

Description

`--command`

The command to run, use message for args

`--continue`

`-c`

Continue the last session

`--session`

`-s`

Session ID to continue

`--fork`

Fork the session when continuing (use with `--continue` or `--session`)

`--share`

Share the session

`--model`

`-m`

Model to use in the form of provider/model

`--agent`

Agent to use

`--file`

`-f`

File(s) to attach to message

`--format`

Format: default (formatted) or json (raw JSON events)

`--title`

Title for the session (uses truncated prompt if no value provided)

`--attach`

Attach to a running opencode server (e.g., [http://localhost:4096](http://localhost:4096))

`--password`

`-p`

Basic auth password (defaults to `OPENCODE_SERVER_PASSWORD`)

`--username`

`-u`

Basic auth username (defaults to `OPENCODE_SERVER_USERNAME` or `opencode`)

`--dir`

Directory to run in, or path on the remote server when attaching

`--port`

Port for the local server (defaults to random port)

`--variant`

Model variant (provider-specific reasoning effort)

`--thinking`

Show thinking blocks

`--auto`

Auto-approve permissions that are not explicitly denied

---

### [serve](#serve)

Start a headless OpenCode server for API access. Check out the [server docs](/docs/server) for the full HTTP interface.

Terminal window

```
opencode serve
```

This starts an HTTP server that provides API access to opencode functionality without the TUI interface. Set `OPENCODE_SERVER_PASSWORD` to enable HTTP basic auth (username defaults to `opencode`).

#### [Flags](#flags-7)

Flag

Description

`--port`

Port to listen on

`--hostname`

Hostname to listen on

`--mdns`

Enable mDNS discovery

`--mdns-domain`

Custom mDNS domain name

`--cors`

Additional browser origin(s) to allow CORS

---

### [session](#session)

Manage OpenCode sessions.

Terminal window

```
opencode session [command]
```

---

#### [list](#list-3)

List all OpenCode sessions.

Terminal window

```
opencode session list
```

##### [Flags](#flags-8)

Flag

Short

Description

`--max-count`

`-n`

Limit to N most recent sessions

`--format`

Output format: table or json (table)

---

#### [delete](#delete)

Delete an OpenCode session.

Terminal window

```
opencode session delete <sessionID>
```

---

### [stats](#stats)

Show token usage and cost statistics for your OpenCode sessions.

Terminal window

```
opencode stats
```

#### [Flags](#flags-9)

Flag

Description

`--days`

Show stats for the last N days (all time)

`--tools`

Number of tools to show (all)

`--models`

Show model usage breakdown (hidden by default). Pass a number to show top N

`--project`

Filter by project (all projects, empty string: current project)

---

### [export](#export)

Export session data as JSON.

Terminal window

```
opencode export [sessionID]
```

If you don’t provide a session ID, you’ll be prompted to select from available sessions.

#### [Flags](#flags-10)

Flag

Description

`--sanitize`

Redact sensitive transcript/file data

---

### [import](#import)

Import session data from a JSON file or OpenCode share URL.

Terminal window

```
opencode import <file>
```

You can import from a local file or an OpenCode share URL.

Terminal window

```
opencode import session.jsonopencode import https://opncd.ai/s/abc123
```

---

### [web](#web)

Start a headless OpenCode server with a web interface.

Terminal window

```
opencode web
```

This starts an HTTP server and opens a web browser to access OpenCode through a web interface. Set `OPENCODE_SERVER_PASSWORD` to enable HTTP basic auth (username defaults to `opencode`).

#### [Flags](#flags-11)

Flag

Description

`--port`

Port to listen on

`--hostname`

Hostname to listen on

`--mdns`

Enable mDNS discovery

`--mdns-domain`

Custom mDNS domain name

`--cors`

Additional browser origin(s) to allow CORS

---

### [acp](#acp)

Start an ACP (Agent Client Protocol) server.

Terminal window

```
opencode acp
```

This command starts an ACP server that communicates via stdin/stdout using nd-JSON.
```

No — it's not just a copy. `opencode export [sessionID]` writes the session out as **structured JSON**, not a markdown transcript:

- Format: JSON serialization of the session's messages/data (machine-readable, round-trippable via `opencode import`)
- `--sanitize` flag redacts sensitive transcript/file data
- Without an ID it prompts you to pick a session from the list

So the `.md` files in your `conversations/` directory aren't produced by `opencode export` — that command outputs `.json`. Your markdown transcripts likely come from a custom export/plugin or a manual save. Worth knowing:

- `export` → JSON (data, not prose) — good for backup/round-trip, not reading
- `conversations/*.md` → human-readable transcript — what you're tracking
- `/share` → hosted link, nothing saved to disk

For "keep track of how I've been using AI," the markdown is the better record.

Here's how I'd design it:

**Directory + naming**
```
conversations/
└── snapshots/
    └── 20260818-1430-refactor-organize-packages-by-business-capability.md
```
Name = timestamp + slug of the commit message (lowercased, non-alphanumerics → `-`, truncated ~50 chars, uniqueness/ordering from the timestamp). Each commit's snapshot is a distinct, human-readable file.

**Mechanism — two options**

*A) A git alias/function wrapping commit (recommended)* — transparent, predictable, easy to tweak:
```zsh
# ~/.zshrc
gc() {
  local msg="$1"
  local session="$(ls -t conversations/*.md 2>/dev/null | head -1)"
  [[ -z "$session" ]] && { git commit -m "$msg"; return; }
  local slug="$(echo "$msg" | tr '[:upper:]' '[:lower:]' \
    | sed 's/[^a-z0-9]\+/-/g; s/^-//; s/-$//' | cut -c1-50)"
  local dest="conversations/snapshots/$(date +%Y%m%d-%H%M%S)-${slug}.md"
  mkdir -p "$(dirname "$dest)" && cp "$session" "$dest" && git add "$dest"
  git commit -m "$msg"
}
```
You then commit as `gc "refactor: fix login bug"` and the snapshot rides along. (Positional-arg style — fine for conventional commits.)

*B) A `prepare-commit-msg` git hook* — fully automatic on plain `git commit`, but it has to stage files mid-commit and derive the name from the message file, which is fragile and surprising when it breaks. I'd avoid it.

**One decision to make first:** the live session file (`conversations/20260818-11-25-13-Greeting.md`) keeps changing every turn, so it shows ` M` on every commit. Options:
- Track **snapshots only**, gitignore the live file (each commit gets one frozen copy — cleanest)
- Track both (commits drag along the live file's changing content too)

My recommendation: snapshots-only. Want me to set this up — which option for the live file, and should it be a `gc` function in your `~/.zshrc`?

**Title:** tail -5 ~/.zshrc; echo "---"; cat >> ~/.zshrc <<'EOF'

# Commit with an AI session transcript snapshot.
# Usage: gc "conventional commit message"
gc() {
    local msg="$1"
    local session
    session="$(ls -t conversations/*.md 2>/dev/null | head -1)"
    if [[ -z "$session" ]]; then
        git commit -m "$msg"
        return
    fi
    local slug
    slug="$(printf '%s' "$msg" | tr '[:upper:]' '[:lower:]' \
        | sed 's/[^a-z0-9]\{1,\}/-/g; s/^-//; s/-$//' | cut -c1-50)"
    local dest="conversations/snapshots/$(date +%Y%m%d-%H%M%S)-${slug}.md"
    mkdir -p "$(dirname "$dest")"
    cp "$session" "$dest"
    git add "$dest"
    git commit -m "$msg"
}
EOF
zsh -n ~/.zshrc && echo "SYNTAX OK"; tail -20 ~/.zshrc
